# PRD: Glam Gills: Find Your Flair — MVP

- Status: Draft
- Date: 2026-10-18
- Game: Glam Gills: Find Your Flair. Short name: Glam Gills, for small spaces such as the phone icon (issue #28).
- Builds on: v0.2.0 swim test
- Related: [ADR 0007](../adr/0007-kid-safety-and-privacy.md), [ADR 0008](../adr/0008-gentle-fail-state.md), [art style guide](../art-style-guide.md)
- Source of truth: this PRD holds every requirement. Issues link to its IDs (for example B4) instead of restating them. A requirement change updates this PRD in the same PR.

## Problem Statement

The v0.2.0 swim test has one canal zone, four screens wide.
Players can eat snacks (worms and bugs) and watch a snack counter go up.
They can ride the current, weave through reeds, pop bubbles, and use Tail Dash.
They steer with an analog swim pad in the bottom-left corner and a Dash button.
The game can be paused, respects reduced motion, and deploys to GitHub Pages.

The swim test proves that moving feels good.
It does not give players a reason to keep playing.
There is nothing to find, nothing at stake, and nothing to keep.

Play-testers also saw an old cached build after a new deploy.
They could not tell which version they were playing.

Why now: the swim test has shipped.
The MVP is the first complete loop of the game.

### Who it's for

| Who | Role | What they need |
|---|---|---|
| Kids aged 8 to 12 | Primary: the player | A calm, fun reason to explore, collect, and come back. |
| Parent or guardian | Secondary | Privacy, trust that the game is safe, and a way to start over. |
| Play-tester or developer | Tertiary | To know which build is running and to get the newest one. |

## Solution

### Goals

| ID | Goal |
|---|---|
| G1 | A reason to explore: hidden treasures to find and stranded axolotls to rescue. |
| G2 | Gentle stakes: dodge invasive tilapia. A hit costs a leg, and the leg grows back. |
| G3 | Risk versus reward: bank finds at the Home Spot, or risk them by exploring further. |
| G4 | Something to keep and show off: a Home Tank with decorations, wearables, rescued axolotls, and a Collection Book with real facts. |
| G5 | Progress is never lost by closing the game or by an update. |
| G6 | Stay kid-safe, per [ADR 0007](../adr/0007-kid-safety-and-privacy.md) and [ADR 0008](../adr/0008-gentle-fail-state.md). |
| G7 | Make it mine: customize your axolotl, dress axolotls in outfits with small perks, and give them Dream colours. |
| G8 | A goal for each area: eat every snack to open the exit, beat the area, and earn up to 3 stars. |

### Core loop

1. First launch only: make a starter axolotl in the character creator, or tap "Surprise me".
2. Start in the Home Tank.
3. Choose "Go exploring". Pick which axolotl goes exploring and check its outfit. Then start the trip.
4. Swim Area 1, the canal, or, once the canal is beaten, Area 2, the lily pad pond, or, once that is beaten, Area 3, the pond at dusk, or, once that is beaten, Area 4, the chinampa gardens, or, once that is beaten, Area 5, the chinampa gardens again (N2): find treasures, rescue an axolotl on some trips, dodge the tilapia (and, in Area 3, two herons, in Area 4, the ghost nets, and, in Area 5, two garter snakes and the ghost nets, and wait for the water hyacinth mats to open), and eat snacks. Eating every snack opens the exit.
5. Bank finds at the Home Spot. Then keep exploring, or go home. Or reach the open exit to beat the area, bank everything, and earn stars. If all four legs are lost, the axolotl is carried home instead, and the snacks start over.
6. Back in the Home Tank, decorate, dress up, restyle, and give Dream colours to the axolotls. Visit the Xochimilco refuge to see the trash each beaten area cleared (N2).
7. Repeat.

### Features

**A. Predator, legs and banking.**
Tilapia patrol the canal. They warn before they lunge, and only one lunges at a time. A lunge that connects costs one leg, shown as a gentle sparkle poof. Legs grow back over time. Eating snacks while a leg is missing makes it grow back faster. Fewer legs means a slower, wobblier swim, but the axolotl can always get home. Finds stay in the Trip Bag until the player banks them at the Home Spot. If the last leg is lost, the axolotl is carried home, and unbanked finds go back into the area. Every eaten snack comes back too, so the snacks start over. Lost treasures are placed on the next trip in this play session, in freshly picked hiding spots.

**B. Hidden treasures and rarity.**
Treasures come in three categories: Decorations, Wearables, and Paint shells. A Paint shell holds one Dream colour. There are 16 Dream colours: 12 in the canal, Lily green in the lily pad pond, Firefly gold in Area 3, the pond at dusk, Poppy red in Area 4, the chinampa gardens, and Plum in Area 5, the chinampa gardens again (B3). Solid Dream colours are Common or Rare. Patterned and sheen Dream colours are always Epic or Legendary, so they are rarer. Each treasure has one of four rarities. Each trip has a set number of treasure slots, and chance picks each slot's rarity, so no trip is sure to hold an Epic or a Legendary. Each slot hides one unbanked treasure. Each one hides in a spot picked from its own pool of hiding places, and the better-hidden spots are picked more often. Rarer treasures turn up on fewer trips. Rarer treasures are hidden better: behind reeds, in a cave or other hollow, past the current, or inside something only Tail Dash can break. Rarer treasures are also harder to get: they sit nearer the tilapia.

**C. Rescue, Home Tank and Collection Book.**
About 4 trips in 10 have one stranded axolotl to rescue. It is always one of the real morphs the player did not choose in the character creator. Rescued axolotls live in the Home Tank. The player places decorations in the tank, dresses axolotls in outfits, and can rename and restyle any axolotl. The Collection Book shows every real morph, with true fun facts about real axolotls. Dream colours are listed separately and marked as not found in nature.

**D. Save and load.**
Progress saves on the device by itself. Closing the game or reloading never loses banked progress. Parents can start over.

**E. Updates.**
After a new version is deployed, players get it quickly and never mid-trip. The version number is visible.

**F. Character creator and outfits.**
At first launch, the player makes a starter axolotl step by step, one choice per screen: one of the eight real morphs, boy or girl, a face, a gill style, spots, a tail, a body, an age, a size, and a name. Every option shows as a big picture button. Size, tail, body, and age are for looks only. The other seven real morphs become the axolotls to rescue. Any owned axolotl can be renamed and restyled in the Home Tank. Its colour and boy or girl are fixed after creation. Each axolotl has eight wearable slots: Head, Neck, Face, Shirt, Back, Tail, Socks, and Feet, with one item per slot. Each wearable gives one small perk. Rarer wearables give a slightly stronger perk and look fancier. Each perk type has a hard cap, so no outfit makes an axolotl untouchable. Before a trip, the player picks which axolotl goes exploring. Only that axolotl's outfit applies on the trip. Dream colours, found in Paint shells, can be put on any owned axolotl and taken off at any time. One owned Dream colour can be on any number of owned axolotls at the same time. Names stay on the device.

**H. Areas, exit and stars.**
Areas are the levels of the game. The canal is Area 1, and it is the only area in the MVP. After the MVP, the lily pad pond is Area 2 (built, issue #75; N2), the pond at dusk is Area 3 (built, issue #98), and the chinampa gardens are Area 4 (built, issue #76), the first area that scrolls up and down, and Area 5 (built, issue #103), the first U-turn area, whose exit is under its start. Each area's exit stays closed until every snack in the area has been eaten. A counter shows how many snacks are left, and the last 3 sparkle. In Area 1, snacks drift slowly. In Area 2, they drift a little faster. In Area 3, they drift a little faster again, and a few dart away a little (H4). In Area 4, they drift a little faster than in Area 3, and a few dart away too. In Area 5, they drift a little faster again, and a few dart away too. Reaching the open exit beats the area and banks everything in the Trip Bag. Beating an area earns up to 3 stars, and stars, once earned, are kept.

**X. Cross-cutting.**
Everything stays calm, never scary, readable without colour, and usable with reduced motion, touch, or keyboard. The world is built from areas so more habitats can be added later.

## User Stories

### A. Predator, legs and banking

- As a player, I want the tilapia to warn me before it lunges, so that I have a fair chance to swim away.
- As a player, I want my lost legs to grow back, so that one mistake does not ruin my trip.
- As a player, I want snacks to help my lost leg grow back, so that I can recover after a hit.
- As a player, I want to bank my finds at the Home Spot, so that I know they are safe.
- As a player, I want to choose whether to keep exploring with unbanked finds, so that I can take a risk for a bigger haul.
- As a player, I want to be carried home gently when I lose all my legs, so that I feel ready to try again, not scared.
- As a parent, I want losing to never feel harsh or gory, so that my child stays calm and happy while playing.

### B. Hidden treasures and rarity

- As a player, I want to find treasures hidden around the canal, so that exploring every corner feels worth it.
- As a player, I want rarer treasures to be harder to find and harder to get, so that finding a Legendary feels special.
- As a player, I want a small hint when I am near a hidden treasure, so that I know where to look closer.
- As a player who cannot tell some colours apart, I want each rarity to have its own badge shape and name, so that I know how rare a treasure is.
- As a player, I want to find Paint shells with Dream colours, so that I can give my axolotls magical colours.

### C. Rescue, Home Tank and Collection Book

- As a player, I want to rescue stranded axolotls, so that my Home Tank fills up with friends.
- As a player, I want to place decorations in my Home Tank, so that it feels like my own place.
- As a player, I want to put hats, bows and glasses on my axolotls, so that I can show them off.
- As a player, I want a Collection Book with fun facts, so that I learn about real axolotls.
- As a player, I want unfound morphs to show a friendly hint, so that I know what to look for next.
- As a player, I want to see every treasure, even ones not found yet, so that I know what to look for next.
- As a player, I want to know what each kind of treasure is for, so that I can use it.

### D. Save and load

- As a player, I want my progress to save by itself, so that I never lose my treasures and axolotls.
- As a parent, I want saves to stay on this device with no account and no personal data, so that my child's privacy is protected.
- As a parent, I want a clear way to start over, so that a sibling or my child can begin fresh.
- As a parent, I want to know where progress is kept, so that I do not erase it by accident when clearing browser data.

### E. Updates

- As a play-tester, I want to see the version number, so that I know which build I am testing.
- As a play-tester, I want to get the new build right after a deploy, so that I do not test an old version.
- As a player, I want updates to never interrupt a trip, so that I do not lose what I am doing.
- As a parent, I want update checks to send no data about my child, so that the game stays private.

### F. Character creator and outfits

- As a player, I want to make my own axolotl when I first play, so that it feels like mine from the start.
- As a player who cannot decide, I want a "Surprise me" choice, so that I can start playing quickly.
- As a player, I want to make my axolotl one step at a time and see every choice as a big picture, so that each choice is easy to understand.
- As a player, I want to choose boy or girl, a tail, a body, and an age, so that my axolotl looks just how I imagine.
- As a player, I want to rename and restyle my rescued axolotls, so that each one is special.
- As a player, I want to choose which axolotl goes exploring and what it wears, so that I can plan my trip.
- As a player, I want each wearable to give a small perk, so that dressing up also helps me on trips.
- As a player, I want to layer a shirt, a necklace and a backpack, and wear socks with shoes, so that my axolotl's outfit looks complete.
- As a player who cannot tell some colours apart, I want each perk shown with an icon and words, so that I know what it does.
- As a parent, I want my child's axolotl names to stay on this device and never be shared, so that nothing personal leaves the game.
- As a parent, I want Dream colours clearly marked as not found in nature, so that my child still learns true facts about axolotls.

### H. Areas, exit and stars

- As a player, I want to beat an area by reaching its exit, so that each area feels like a level I can finish.
- As a player, I want to earn stars in an area, so that I have a reason to replay it.
- As a player, I want to see how many snacks are left, so that I know how close the exit is to opening.
- As a player, I want the last snacks to sparkle, so that I can find them.

### X. Cross-cutting

- As a player who prefers less motion, I want calm, still versions of animations, so that I can play comfortably.
- As a player, I want to play with touch or with a keyboard, so that I can use whatever device I have.
- As a parent, I want no ads, chat, purchases or tracking, so that the game is safe for my child.
- As a player, I want to swim to new areas in future versions, so that the world keeps growing.

## Acceptance Criteria

### A. Predator, legs and banking

1. **A1** Each area's predators, and how many of each, are listed in its danger pass paragraph in N2 (issue #117). They are spread along the area, each patrolling its own route within the water. None ever enters the Home Spot safe area, chasing or not. Only one predator telegraphs or lunges at a time, across every predator in the area, whatever its species, and a way home is always open (N3; open question 118). Chasing is not attacking: any number of predators may chase at once (N3, the chase rule; open question 249); only the warning and the strike are one at a time. A moving hazard, such as the punting pole (N3), is not a predator, so it is outside this one-at-a-time rule: its fixed cycle is always on show, and it may jab while a predator warns or strikes (open question 255).
2. **A2** Every lunge is preceded by a telegraph (a glint and a short wind-up pose). The telegraph lasts at least the minimum wind-up time. It can be read without colour and with reduced motion, using a non-motion cue such as a pose change or a "!" icon. A moving hazard's jab is preceded by its own telegraph, which also lasts at least the minimum wind-up time and touches nothing: for the punting pole, the boat's shadow darkens on the water under its bow, and the pole's tip dips to the surface with a ripple ring, for 1 second. In greyscale the shadow and the ring have an ink outline, and under reduced motion they show still; the jab itself still moves, because it is gameplay (N3; open questions 255 and 256).
3. **A3** A lunge starts only when the axolotl is within lunge range. A predator whose tuning has a chase may chase before it lunges: on patrol, it swims toward an exposed axolotl in sight, on its leash, no nearer than its lunge range, and warns from there by the usual rules (N3, the chase rule; open question 249). After a lunge, a cooldown applies before the next telegraph.
4. **A4** A hit is a lunge that connects. It removes exactly one leg. Touching a tilapia, or any other predator, that is not lunging only bumps the axolotl gently away and costs no leg (see open question 1). Touching a moving hazard while it is in the water (jabbing, holding, or lifting) costs one leg: it is a hit by the same rules (A5, A11, A22) and is never a bump (N3; open question 255).
5. **A5** After a hit, the axolotl has protected time. No second hit can land during it. The cue is steady and non-flashing (for example, a soft shimmer) and is static under reduced motion.
6. **A6** Leg loss shows a sparkle "poof". It never shows blood, wounds, or a detached limb. The lost leg shows as a faint outline.
7. **A7** Each lost leg regrows after about 20 seconds, one leg at a time. Regrowth is shown with sparkles and the outline filling in.
8. **A8** The legs display shows four leg icons. Each icon is present, regrowing (with progress), or lost. The states are told apart by shape or fill, not by colour alone.
9. **A9** Fewer legs means a lower top speed and a wobblier swim. Wobble is suppressed under reduced motion; the slowdown stays. With 1 to 4 legs, the axolotl can always swim against every current and reach the Home Spot from anywhere. Area 3's stream is the strongest current so far, 74 pixels per second, so a one-leg axolotl still makes 16 pixels per second of headway against it (see Tuning values). One exception: while queasy from a plastic worm (N3), its top speed is 60% for up to 3 seconds, so its headway is lower for that time. Plastic worms start far enough from every current (see Tuning values) that a queasy one-leg axolotl never loses headway in one (open question 149). Another exception: while snagged on a ghost net (N3), its top speed is 50% for up to 2 seconds, so a snagged one-leg axolotl swims 45 pixels per second, under Area 4's 72 pixel per second current. Ghost nets sit far enough from every current (at least 190.5 pixels, see Tuning values) that a snag always ends before the axolotl reaches one (open question 195). A drifting ghost net (N3) keeps its whole drift path at least 190.5 pixels from every current, so this holds wherever it has drifted to (open question 258). Area 4's current leaves a one-leg axolotl 18 pixels per second of headway.
10. **A10** Tail Dash works with any number of legs. Being queasy or snagged (N3) never slows Tail Dash: only the top speed drops, and the acceleration and every Tail Dash value stay the same (open questions 149 and 193).
11. **A11** Losing the fourth leg ends the trip. The axolotl is carried home to the Home Tank with a gentle transition (for example, it floats home in a bubble; a still card under reduced motion). If reduced motion turns on during the carry, the still version takes over at once, and if it turns off, the axolotl floats home from where it is (open question 59). The tilapia is never shown catching or eating the axolotl. There is no "game over". A kind message says what was lost and that everything banked is safe.
12. **A12** Being carried home is a full reset of the trip. All unbanked trip finds leave the Trip Bag and go back into the area of the trip. In this play session, they are always placed on the next trip in that area, each in a freshly picked valid hiding spot (B4). After the game is closed or reloaded, they rejoin their area and can turn up on a later trip (D3). Every snack eaten comes back, so the snacks start over on the next trip (H2). Banked items and stars are untouched. The axolotl arrives with four legs. (Product owner decision, issue #31. Review after play-tests; see open question 15.)
13. **A13** A clearly marked Home Spot sits where each trip starts. It is a safe area.
14. **A14** Reaching the Home Spot banks every trip find at once, with a short celebration that lists them. The Trip Bag empties. The banked total rises by exactly the number of finds banked.
15. **A15** At the Home Spot, the player can keep exploring or go home. Going home banks first. Both choices work by touch and by keyboard.
16. **A16** The Trip Bag display shows the number of unbanked finds and that they are "not safe yet" (an icon and a number).
17. **A17** Pause freezes the tilapia, the herons (their wading, warning, stab, and cooldown, N3), telegraphs, cooldowns, protected time, regrowth, queasy time (N3), snag time (N3), chases (N3), moving hazards and their cycle (N3), drifting ghost nets (N3), tadpoles on their lanes, and shrimp flicks and rests (H4; issue #117).
18. **A18** Every trip starts at the Home Spot with four legs and an empty Trip Bag.
19. **A19** Snacks are not trip finds. The axolotl eats a snack when its head, where it is drawn, touches the snack where it is drawn (with its drift and bob, H4), under full and reduced motion. The eat reach is the same at every size, so size stays for looks only (F4; see Tuning values). With four legs, eating a snack does not change the legs. While a leg is missing, each snack eaten moves the regrowing leg's regrowth forward by a fixed amount (see Tuning values). Regrowth only moves forward and never goes past a full leg. The player gets clear feedback when a snack speeds up regrowth, readable in greyscale and under reduced motion. The speed-up works together with the Regrowth perk. A tadpole or a freshwater shrimp (H4) is eaten where it is drawn, like any snack: a tadpole where it is on its lane, and a shrimp with its drift, bob, and flick. A Snack magnet perk pulls each snack within its radius of the explorer's head toward the head at a gentle speed, fixed for each area (F9; see Tuning values), so the explorer still eats it by touching it; the eat reach never changes. The pull is max(40, 1.2 × the top speed of the area's fastest mobile snack) pixels per second, so it always closes on every snack in the area, and an area with no mobile snacks keeps 40 (issue #117; open question 275). A plastic worm (N3) is never a snack: it never counts toward opening the exit, and the Snack magnet never pulls it. Banking does not affect snacks. Being carried home resets them (A12). Every snack must be eaten to open the exit (H2). (Issue #30. This replaces "snacks keep their swim-test behaviour".)
20. **A20** The first trip shows one short hint: bring finds to the Home Spot to keep them safe.
21. **A21** The first time the player meets the tilapia, a short fact card appears with play paused. The player can dismiss it. It explains that the tilapia is a fish people brought to Xochimilco, that it does not belong there, and that it eats axolotl eggs and food. The tone is gentle, with no blame. The card can be read again in the Collection Book once the book exists.
22. **A22** Perks from the explorer's outfit can change the rules for speed, regrowth, Tail Dash, treasure hints, snacks, and blocking a hit, only within the per-type caps (see F9 to F12). With any outfit, every hit that a Shell perk does not block still removes exactly one leg, and losing the fourth leg still ends the trip (A11). A Shell perk blocks the first hit of the trip. A hit blocked by a Shell perk costs no leg, shows no leg-loss puff, and starts normal protected time (A5). It shows a short Shell sparkle round the axolotl instead, which is still under reduced motion; the legs display does not change (open question 72).

### B. Hidden treasures and rarity

1. **B1** There are three treasure categories: Decorations (Home Tank items, for example plants, castles, and shells), Wearables (for example hats, bows, and glasses), and Paint shells. A Paint shell holds one Dream colour. Banking a Paint shell makes its Dream colour owned. Paint shells are hidden, found, and banked like other treasures.
2. **B2** There are four rarities: Common, Rare, Epic, and Legendary, in that order. Each has its own colour, its own distinct rounded badge shape, and its name in text wherever it is shown. Each can be identified in greyscale. Common is green (#1fb98d; open question 83). Each rarity's colour also shows as a faint glow behind its treasures (open questions 86 to 88). The glow uses the same colour as the rarity's badge, so one rarity always has one colour. Colour stays the weakest cue: the badge shape and the rarity name still carry rarity, and the glow never replaces them (X2).
3. **B3** The MVP catalogue, the canal's, has these minimums:
    - Decorations: at least one at every rarity.
    - Wearables: at least one at every rarity, and at least one for each slot (Head, Neck, Face, Shirt, Back, Tail, Socks, and Feet).
    - Paint shells: exactly twelve in the canal, one for each of the canal's 12 Dream colours, at these rarities:
        - Common: Mint, Sky blue, and Lavender (solid).
        - Rare: Sunset orange, Bubblegum, and Midnight blue (solid).
        - Epic: Hearts, Tie-dye, and Iridescent (patterned or sheen).
        - Legendary: Rainbow, Galaxy, and Tie-dye galaxy (patterned).
    - Rule: patterned and sheen Dream colours are always Epic or Legendary. Solid Dream colours are Common or Rare.
    - In total, 12 or more Decorations and Wearables (at least 4 Decorations and 8 Wearables), plus the twelve Paint shells. The canal has 24 treasures.

    The lily pad pond, Area 2, adds 12 new treasures (built, issue #75). Each pond Wearable has one existing perk at the canal's value for its rarity (F9, Tuning values; open question 146):
    - Decorations: Lily pad (Common, Floating), Cattail clump (Common, Floor), Frog statue (Rare, Floor; the pond's special treasure, H6), and Lotus lantern (Epic, Floating).
    - Wearables: Lily flower crown (Head, Common, Regrowth 10%), Pond-weed scarf (Neck, Common, Snack magnet 30), Dragonfly glasses (Face, Rare, Treasure sense 25%), Raincoat (Shirt, Rare, Speed 6%), Ladybug backpack (Back, Epic, Shell 1 hit), Rainbow dragonfly wings (Back, Legendary, Dash 25%), and Froggy boots (Feet, Legendary, Speed 10%).
    - Paint shells: one, the Lily green Paint shell (Rare). Lily green is a solid Dream colour, the 13th (open question 148).

    The lily pad pond at dusk, Area 3, adds 12 more treasures (built, issue #98). Their ids start with "pond/", like Area 2's (open question 161). Each Area 3 Wearable has one existing perk at the canal's value for its rarity (F9, Tuning values; open question 167):
    - Decorations: Reed bundle (Common, Floor), Drifting feather (Common, Floating), Wooden duck decoy (Rare, Floating; Area 3's special treasure, H6), Firefly string lights (Rare, Hanging, 144 units wide; open question 162), Firefly jar (Epic, Hanging), and Golden lotus throne (Legendary, Floor).
    - Wearables: Feather cap (Head, Common, Speed 4%), Lily-pad socks (Socks, Common, Snack magnet 30), Water-lily shirt (Shirt, Rare, Regrowth 15%), Pearl tail charm (Tail, Epic, Shell 1 hit), and Firefly cape (Back, Legendary, Treasure sense 50%).
    - Paint shells: one, the Firefly gold Paint shell (Rare). Firefly gold is a solid Dream colour, the 14th (open question 175).

    The chinampa gardens, Area 4, add 12 more treasures (built, issue #76), all new, with ids that start with "chinampa/". Each Area 4 Wearable has one existing perk at the canal's value for its rarity (F9, Tuning values; open question 201):
    - Common: Clay flowerpot (Decoration, Floor), Corn husk doll (Decoration, Floor), Straw sun hat (Wearable, Head, Treasure sense 15%), and Marigold necklace (Wearable, Neck, Regrowth 10%).
    - Rare: Scarecrow (Decoration, Floor; Area 4's special treasure, H6), Papel picado banner (Decoration, Hanging, 144 units wide; open question 200), Garden overalls (Wearable, Shirt, Snack magnet 40), and the Poppy red Paint shell. Poppy red is a solid Dream colour, the 15th, and its name clashes with no other Dream colour (open question 202).
    - Epic: Willow swing (Decoration, Hanging) and Monarch hair clip (Wearable, Head, Dash 20%).
    - Legendary: Golden corn (Decoration, Floor) and Monarch wings (Wearable, Back, Speed 10%).

    The chinampa gardens' Area 5 adds 12 more treasures (built, issue #103), all new, in their own list after Area 4's, with ids that start with "chinampa/", like Area 4's (open question 221). Each Area 5 Wearable has one existing perk at the canal's value for its rarity (F9, Tuning values; open question 239):
    - Common: Watering can (Decoration, Floor), Floating gourd (Decoration, Floating), Bee headband (Wearable, Head, Snack magnet 30), and Bandana (Wearable, Neck, Speed 4%).
    - Rare: Piñata (Decoration, Hanging; Area 5's special treasure, H6), Sunflower sunglasses (Wearable, Face, Treasure sense 25%), Embroidered shirt (Wearable, Shirt, Regrowth 15%), and the Plum Paint shell. Plum is a solid Dream colour, the 16th, a light orchid, and its name clashes with no other Dream colour (open question 240).
    - Epic: Talavera fountain (Decoration, Floor) and Hummingbird tail ribbon (Wearable, Tail, Speed 8%).
    - Legendary: Alebrije statue (Decoration, Floor) and Rainbow serape cape (Wearable, Back, Shell 1 hit).

    The whole catalogue is the canal's treasures, then the pond's, then Area 3's, then Area 4's, then Area 5's: 72 treasures, which are 25 Decorations, 31 Wearables, and 16 Paint shells, for 16 Dream colours. More of any rarity can be added later as area data (B4).

    Each item has a unique name, a category, and a rarity. Each Wearable also has a slot and a perk, and may have a set ID (Implementation Decision 12). No item can be owned twice.
4. **B4** Each treasure has a pool of hiding spots, and every spot in it meets that treasure's rarity rule (B6). At the start of each trip, the trip seed fills the area's treasure slots (issue #94; see Tuning values):
    - Each area has a set number of treasure slots per trip. The canal has 6, the lily pad pond has 7 (issue #75), Area 3 has 8 (issue #98), Area 4 has 9 (issue #76), and Area 5 has 10 (issue #103; the owner's number; open question 238).
    - The trip seed picks each slot's rarity on its own: Common 65%, Rare 27%, Epic 6%, and Legendary 2%. No trip is sure to hold an Epic or a Legendary.
    - A slot takes an unbanked treasure of its rarity that is not already on the trip.
    - If none of that rarity is left, the slot takes a more common rarity that has one left, nearest first. If no more common rarity has one, it takes only the next rarer one: Common to Rare, Rare to Epic, or Epic to Legendary, never two or more steps rarer. A rarity whose spot pool has no free spot counts as none left (open question 140). A slot with nothing left at any of those rarities stays empty and draws nothing. So a Legendary comes only from a slot that rolled Legendary, or from an Epic slot once the area's Epics are all banked (owner decision on issue #111, 2026-10-22; open question 62).
    - When only Common and Rare treasures are left unbanked and there are enough slots for all of them, all of them are placed, whatever rarity each slot rolled. An unbanked Epic can only be placed by a Rare, Epic, or Legendary slot, and an unbanked Legendary only by an Epic or Legendary slot, so they may wait for a later trip.
    - The area's special treasure (H6) is always placed while it is unbanked, as an extra on top of the slots.
    - Lost treasures (A12) are always placed on the next trip in this play session, as extras on top of the slots. They do not use up a slot.
    - The slot rarities, the treasure picks, and the stranded axolotl's chance (C2) each draw from their own stream of the trip seed (open question 137).
    - Every area uses these numbers unless it sets its own slot count and chances. The catalogue can hold any number of treasures of any rarity, as area data.

    Each picked treasure then gets a spot picked from its own pool. Spots farther from the main swim path are picked more often, but any spot in the pool can still be picked. No two treasures share a spot in one trip. A Legendary always lands in a Dash pot in a cave or other hollow, or past the current (B6). Hiding spots shuffle each trip (product owner decision, 2026-10-18, issue #11; this replaces "one fixed spot"). Treasure slots are a product owner decision, 2026-10-21 (issue #94; this replaces the fixed mix from issue #39 of 2 Common, 1 Rare, 1 Epic, and 1 Legendary, with Rare on 80% of trips, Epic on 50%, and Legendary on 25%; see open questions 60 to 66 and 137 to 140). The one-step fallback is a product owner decision, 2026-10-22 (issue #111; this replaces "then the next rarer one", which let a Common slot reach a Legendary).
5. **B5** Hiding places include behind reeds, under lily pads, under an overhanging chinampa bank, inside a small cave or other hollow (in the lily pad pond, a hollow log; in Area 3, a root arch under the bank; in Area 4, a tangle of ahuejote roots), and past the current. In Areas 2 and 3, two fixed lily pads hold hiding spots, one Common and one Rare, and the other lily pads, some with flowers, are scenery only. In Area 4, the Common and Rare spots are under the overhanging banks of the garden plots: the water under each top-row bank, from the surface down to y 140, hides what is in it (the bank cover; issue #76; open question 197). Six Common spots sit under the first banks, near the main swim path, and six Rare spots under the bank in the top right, a dead end off the path. Area 4 has no reeds and no lily pads. Area 5 (issue #103) hides its Common and Rare spots the same way, under the overhanging banks of its top-row garden plots, from the surface down to y 140: six Common spots under the first two banks, near the main swim path, and eight Rare spots off it, two at the right end of the first bank and six under the bank in the top right, a dead end (open question 235). Area 5 has no reeds and no lily pads either. Some treasures sit inside a container that only Tail Dash can break open. Swimming into the container without dashing shows a "dash needed" hint and does not open it. Breaking it is a gentle crumble with sparkles and no sudden flash.
6. **B6** Rarer treasures are hidden better. Common treasures can be reached from the main swim path. Rare treasures need a detour. Epic treasures are in a cave or other hollow, or past the current. Legendary treasures need Tail Dash and are in a cave or other hollow, or past the current. In the lily pad pond, the hollow log counts as the cave, and the stream flowing into the pond at its right end is the current, with spots past it (issue #75). In Area 3, the root arch under the bank counts as the cave, and the stream flowing into the pond near its far end, from under the bank roots, is the current, with spots past it (issue #98). In Area 4, the tangle of ahuejote roots on the bed of the bottom row, in the bottom left, counts as the cave, with Legendary Dash pots near its top and Epic spots lower down; the water flowing sideways through the gap between two chinampas in the bottom row is the current, with spots just past it (issue #76). In Area 5, a tangle of ahuejote roots on the bed of the bottom row counts as the cave, a dead end below the main path, with 2 Legendary Dash pots and 2 Epic spots; the water flowing right through the gap between two chinampas in the bottom row, against the main path where the path crosses it, is the current, with 2 Legendary Dash pots and 2 Epic spots just past it (issue #103). A spot is past a current when it is within 140 pixels beyond the current's upstream edge, the edge its flow comes from, and inside the current's y range; a current with no sideways flow has no past side (open question 223). In Areas 1 to 4 every current flows left with home to its left, so their spots past the current are on the side away from home, as before; Area 5's current flows right, so its spots past the current are on its left, the far side along the main path. Paint shells follow these same rules for their rarity, so rarer Dream colours are hidden better. In an area with a threat (a predator, or a hazard that costs a leg), every spot must also meet the risk ladder (B10).
7. **B7** Near a hidden treasure, a subtle hint appears that does not rely on colour (a small sparkle). Rarer treasures have a subtler, shorter-range hint. A Treasure sense perk makes the hint appear from further away: each hint range is multiplied by 1 plus the perk's total (for example, +35% gives 1.35 times the range), within its cap (see F9 and F11). The hint is static under reduced motion. The hint sparkle stays the only cue for a hidden treasure: a hidden treasure never glows (B2, B8).
8. **B8** Swimming into a revealed treasure adds it to the Trip Bag, with a short toast at a screen edge showing its name, category, and rarity badge with the rarity name. At most one toast shows at a time; a newer find replaces it (X10). A revealed treasure (one a crumbled Dash pot shows) glows in its rarity's colour (B2), and so does its pickup pop under full motion. A hidden treasure, under cover, never glows, even while its hint sparkle shows (B7; open question 84). On a trip, in every area, the glow breathes slowly, once every 2.4 seconds, and it is static under reduced motion (open question 87).
9. **B9** Banked treasures are owned forever and are never placed again. A hiding spot holds whatever treasure this trip picked for it, or nothing. Unbanked treasures lost when the axolotl is carried home are placed on the next trip in this play session, in a freshly picked valid spot. Every catalogue treasure can turn up over many trips. The area's special treasure is always placed while it is unbanked, on top of the treasure slots (B4).
10. **B10** Rarer treasures are harder to get: they sit nearer a threat. A threat is a predator's patrol route, with its lunge (strike) range, or a hazard that costs a leg, with its shape and its reach. A hazard that only slows is not a threat. A spot's distance to a predator is its route distance: the shortest distance from the spot to any point of the patrol route, the stretch from the last turn back to the first included. A spot's distance to a hazard is the shortest distance to the edge of its shape (0 inside it). Each threat has a strike range S: a predator's lunge range, or a hazard's reach. Each threat puts a spot in one risk band (the risk ladder), from least to most risky:
    - Common: farther than S + 60 pixels.
    - Rare: more than S and at most S + 60 pixels.
    - Epic: more than S / 2 and at most S pixels, so within strike range.
    - Legendary: at most S / 2 pixels, and in a Dash pot (B6).

    The tilapia's lunge range is 140 pixels (see Tuning values), so for the tilapia the bands are exactly: Common farther than 200 pixels from every route, Rare more than 140 and at most 200, Epic more than 70 and at most 140, and Legendary at most 70. A spot's band is the riskiest band any one threat puts it in (open question 123). When every threat has the same strike range, that is the band of the shortest distance to any threat. Every spot is at least 60 pixels from every point where a predator turns. Every spot a treasure can hide in must be fair: it can be reached and left with no hit, by timing the patrol, with four legs. One Tail Dash is allowed, only to break a Legendary's pot. On top of the per-trip spot picks (B4), spots on the risky side of each pool are picked more often, by the shortest distance to any threat: nearer for Rare, Epic, and Legendary, and farther for Common. Any spot in the pool can still be picked, and the same trip seed gives the same spots. An area with no threat uses B6 alone. The canal's spots do not change; the generic measure matters from area 2 on. In the lily pad pond (Area 2), plastic worms only slow (N3), so they are not threats, and the 4 tilapia are its only threats. Every pond spot meets its rarity's band and the turn clearance from the tilapia routes, and the fairness check holds for every pond spot as for the canal's (issue #75). In Area 3, the 4 tilapia and the 2 herons are its threats, and its plastic worms only slow. Each heron's strike range S is 110 pixels, its reach from where it stands on its stretch (see Tuning values), so for a heron the bands are Common farther than 170 pixels from its stretch, Rare more than 110 and at most 170, Epic more than 55 and at most 110, and Legendary at most 55. A spot's distance to a heron is its route distance to the heron's stretch, as for the tilapia. A heron warns from where it stands on its stretch, so every point it can warn at is within S of its stretch (open question 215). Every Epic and Legendary spot in Area 3 is in the root arch or past the stream, within a heron's reach of its stretch; every Common and Rare spot is more than 110 pixels from every stretch. The risk ladder measures distance only: a heron cannot see an axolotl in cover (N3), but a spot in cover, such as one in the root arch, still counts as near a heron, because getting in and out of it crosses the heron's reach. Treasure spots may still sit within a heron's reach (owner decision on issue #111, 2026-10-23; open question 207). Every Area 3 spot is at least 60 pixels from every tilapia turn and from both ends of each heron's stretch, and the fairness check holds for every Area 3 spot (issues #98 and #111; Testing Decisions). In Area 4, the ghost nets only slow (N3), so they are not threats, and the 2 tilapia are its only threats. Area 4's tilapia play its own tuning (N2, the predator ladder), so their strike range S is its lunge range, 145 pixels, and the bands come from that S: Common farther than 205 pixels from every route, Rare more than 145 and at most 205, Epic more than 72.5 and at most 145, and Legendary at most 72.5. Every Area 4 spot is at least 60 pixels from every tilapia turn, and the fairness check, played with Area 4's own tilapia tuning, holds for every Area 4 spot, on the default seed and on other seeds (issue #76; Testing Decisions; open question 192). In Area 5 (issue #103), the 2 tilapia and the 2 garter snakes are its threats; the ghost nets only slow, and the water hyacinth mats never cost a leg (N3), so neither is a threat. Area 5's tilapia play its own tuning, so their S is 150 pixels: Common farther than 210 pixels from every route, Rare more than 150 and at most 210, Epic more than 75 and at most 150, and Legendary at most 75. Each garter snake's S is its lunge range, 110 pixels, measured as route distance to its stretch, as for the heron: Common farther than 170 pixels, Rare more than 110 and at most 170, Epic more than 55 and at most 110, and Legendary at most 55. Every Area 5 spot meets its rarity's band against every threat, is at least 60 pixels from every tilapia turn and from both ends of each snake's stretch, and the fairness check, played with Area 5's own tilapia tuning and both snakes, holds for every Area 5 spot, on the default seed and on other seeds (Testing Decisions; open question 235). The hint sparkle (B7) and the rarity glow (issue #68) do not change. (Product owner decision, 2026-10-20, issue #70; generic measure from issue #73; see open questions 78 to 82, 120, and 123.)

### C. Rescue, Home Tank and Collection Book

1. **C1** The player starts with one axolotl, made in the character creator (F1). Its morph is the real morph the player chose. It is already in the Home Tank, and its morph is already found in the Collection Book.
2. **C2** Each trip has at most one stranded axolotl. In every area, the trip seed puts one on 40% of trips, about 4 trips in 10, unless one is already waiting (C5; issue #94; open question 138). 40% is the default for every area that sets no chance of its own, and no area sets one: Area 5 (issue #103) uses it too. An area planned at "30%" uses this default unless the owner sets a value for it (owner decision on issue #111, 2026-10-22, which replaces 30%). This chance has its own stream of the trip seed, so a trip seed that has a stranded axolotl still picks the same morph, place, and boy or girl as before. It is always a real morph not yet owned: one of the seven morphs not chosen in the character creator (F5). It looks a little lost, never hurt.
3. **C3** Reaching the stranded axolotl makes it follow the player. It counts as a trip find until banked. The tilapia cannot hit it. It keeps up with the player and never gets stuck.
4. **C4** Banking or going home moves the rescued axolotl into the Home Tank for good. Its morph unlocks in the Collection Book. It gets the default look (open question 22) with its own boy or girl (C5), and the first suggested name that fits its boy or girl and that no owned axolotl uses (F17). The player can restyle it (F6).
5. **C5** A stranded axolotl gets its boy or girl from the trip seed when it is picked. It keeps it while it waits and when it is rescued. If the player is carried home first, the stranded axolotl returns to where it was found. A kind message uses its name and he or she (for example "Gilly swam back to where you found him. He's waiting for you!"). A stranded axolotl left waiting (met but not banked) always comes back on the next trip in that area, with no new roll, so the message keeps its promise. The 40% chance (C2) applies only when nobody is waiting. After the game is closed or reloaded, nobody is waiting (D2), so the next trip rolls (open question 139).
6. **C6** After all seven morphs are rescued, no more stranded axolotls appear in the MVP (see open question 5). The 40% chance (C2) does not change this: only morphs the player does not own yet are stranded, so there are at most 7 rescues.
7. **C7** After the title screen, the game opens in the Home Tank, which is the hub. On first launch, the character creator comes first (F1). A clear "Go exploring" button starts the Go exploring flow (F7). The hub has five actions, in focus and layout order: Go exploring, Decorate, Treasure Chest, Collection Book, and Refuge, which opens the Xochimilco refuge (N2). Refuge is a 92 x 64 tile at full size. Choosing an axolotl in the tank opens its choices card (F6). The Home Tank's controls keep clear of the tank's top band, where Floating and Hanging Decorations sit (C9): they go in the wall above the tank or in the open water below that band, and only where full size does not fit do Decorate, Treasure Chest, Collection Book, Refuge, and Put away become compact 48 x 48 icon-only tiles, with a caption naming the focused tile, while Go exploring keeps its label (open questions 143 and 144). Refuge's icon is a notched lily pad over a wave, in ink only. With five actions the hub is compact on more canvases, by the same rule (open question 186).
    - Reach: where the compact rows fit no room, they try the reach rooms that the decorate tray uses (C9), over the floor Decorations and never into the top band, before stacking from the top of the tallest room. When the hub reaches, the "Home Tank" name panel never shows. Measured, it reaches only at 460 and 480 wide by 320, 340, 360, 375, and 414 tall. At 460 x 320, Go exploring and three compact tiles sit in a row from y 108 to 172, and Refuge sits alone under them at x 206, y 184.
    - Best-stars badge: when the hub reaches, the badge always uses its stacked layout, with the area label above the stars (about 104 x 78.5, with the same 18 pixel text and 24 pixel stars), in the bottom-right corner, never centred (owner ruling A on issue #74, 2026-10-22; open question 178). The focus caption, measured at its drawn size with its padding, stays clear of the badge by 17 pixels at 460 wide and 27 pixels at 480 wide.
8. **C8** Every owned axolotl swims around the tank on its own. Under reduced motion, they hold still poses.
9. **C9** The player can place an owned Decoration in the tank, move it, and put it away, by touch and by keyboard. Each Decoration can be placed at most once. Decorations cannot go outside the tank. Each Decoration has one placement kind, set in the catalogue (issue #95):
    - Floor (the default): it stands on the gravel and moves left, right, and a little up and down.
    - Floating: it rests on the water surface and moves left and right only.
    - Hanging: it hangs from the glass top and moves left and right only.

    All four canal Decorations are Floor. Of the pond's four, the Lily pad and the Lotus lantern are Floating, and the Cattail clump and the Frog statue are Floor (issue #75). Of Area 3's six, the Reed bundle and the Golden lotus throne are Floor, the Drifting feather and the Wooden duck decoy are Floating, and the Firefly jar and the Firefly string lights are Hanging, for 14 Decorations (issue #98). Of Area 4's six, the Clay flowerpot, the Corn husk doll, the Scarecrow, and the Golden corn are Floor, and the Papel picado banner and the Willow swing are Hanging (issue #76). Of Area 5's five, the Watering can, the Talavera fountain, and the Alebrije statue are Floor, the Floating gourd is Floating, and the Piñata is Hanging, all at the standard width, so 25 Decorations ship (issue #103; open question 239). The Firefly string lights are the first wide Decoration to ship, 144 units wide, so their base x runs from 96 to 384 (open questions 142 and 162). The Papel picado banner is the second, also 144 units wide, from base x 96 to 384; the Willow swing has the standard width (open question 200). A Decoration floating in the middle of the water is out of scope. Floating items bob gently and Hanging items sway gently about their hook; the Pond plant's sway is unchanged. Under reduced motion every Decoration holds still at once. Axolotls always swim in front of every kind. A Decoration can be wider than the standard one; its footprint, overlap, suggested spots, focus rings, placement ghost and "no" icon, and rarity glow follow its width (open question 142). By keyboard, Left and Right move a Floating or Hanging Decoration along its line; Up and Down do nothing and show no invalid cue (open question 141). By touch, a dragged Floating or Hanging Decoration stays on its line and stops at the tank sides. The Home Tank's controls keep clear of the top band where Floating and Hanging Decorations sit (C7, open question 143). A tray of 14 Decorations, all that shipped before Area 4, uses the rule for a tray of more than 4. Its rows are centred and may reach over the floor band, never into the top keep-clear band. Rows that fit no usual room try the reach rooms, the wall down to a gap above the top band and then the open water over the floor Decorations; a tray that fits neither goes in pages (below). At 340 x 440, Done sits alone in the open water below the top band. The decorate caption keeps its rule: it shows only where it has room under the controls. With all 14 in the tray it hides at 460 x 320 and 320 x 440, and shows at 340 x 440 (open questions 144, 153, and 171).

    Tray paging (owner decision on issue #76, 2026-10-23; open question 198): a tray that fits neither the usual rooms nor the reach rooms goes in pages. A page holds at most two rows of tray squares, as many squares a row as fit across, and one row where two do not fit. Under them, Back and Next (the Treasure Chest's page buttons; compact, 48 x 48 icon-only tiles named by the decorate caption) sit in one row with Put away and Done, by the same order of tries. They turn the pages, wrapping past either end. Every target stays at least 48 x 48, nothing goes into the top band, and paging keeps working as more Decorations ship. Back and Next show only when there is more than one page; a tray that fits on one page keeps its layout exactly. With the 20 Decorations shipped up to Area 4 the tray paged only for 18 to 20 at 360 x 440 and 375 x 440, and for 19 or 20 at 460 and 480 wide by 320, 340, 360, and 375 tall, and at 480 x 414. With the 25 shipped with Area 5 (issue #103), the tray pages for 18 or more at 360 x 440 and 375 x 440; for 19 or more at 460 and 480 wide by 320, 340, 360, and 375 tall, and at 480 x 414; for 22 or more at 320 x 440, 320 x 480, 390 x 440, 430 x 440, and 520 wide by 320, 340, 360, and 375 tall; for 23 or more at 340 x 440, 340 x 480, 360 x 480, and 375 x 480; and for 25 at 568 wide by 320, 340, 360, and 375 tall. At 460 x 320 a page is two rows of 7 at y 108 and 168, so 20 took two pages, 14 and 6, and 25 take two pages, 14 and 11; Back is at x 85, Next at x 145, and Put away at x 205, all at y 236, and Done at x 265, y 228, its bottom at 292, inside the 304 limit; the caption has no place, so it hides. At 360 x 440 a page is two rows of 5 on the wall at y 16 and 76, so 25 take three pages, 10, 10, and 5; Back is at x 35, Next at x 95, and Put away at x 155, all at y 271, and Done at x 215, y 263, in the open water under the top band, with the caption under them at y 339. At 320 x 440 a page is two rows of 5 on the wall at y 16 and 76, so 25 take three pages, 10, 10, and 5; Back is at x 76, Next at x 136, and Put away at x 196, all at y 136, and Done at x 105, y 284, in the open water; the caption has no place, so it hides. Every page uses a full page's row tops, so the control row holds still as the pages turn. The page clamps when the tray changes, and putting a Decoration away turns to the page of its square. Focus order: the shown page's squares, Back, Next, the placed Decorations, Put away, and Done.

    The save keeps only each placed Decoration's id and place; its kind comes from the catalogue, so existing saves load unchanged (open question 145).
10. **C10** The player can put an owned Wearable on any owned axolotl and take it off, on the restyle screen's Outfit tab (F6). The axolotl choices card's "Outfit" opens it directly, and the explorer card's "Change outfit" still opens it too (F7). Each axolotl has eight slots (Head, Neck, Face, Shirt, Back, Tail, Socks, and Feet) and wears at most one Wearable per slot (F8). Each Wearable fits one slot and is on at most one axolotl at a time: putting on a Wearable that another axolotl wears takes it off that axolotl. Wearables never cover the eyes, the gill roots, or a girl's cheek heart (F13), per the accessory rules in the [art style guide](../art-style-guide.md).
11. **C11** The Treasure Chest lists every catalogue treasure, found or not, in three sections: Decorations, Wearables, and Dream colours, each in catalogue order (open question 89). A found treasure shows its name and rarity badge, with its rarity glow (B2). Each found Wearable also shows its slot and its perk, as the perk icon and text with its value (F10). Each found Dream colour shows its pattern cue (solid colours show their name beside the swatch, F15) and the label "Dream colour — not found in nature". An unfound treasure shows a flat silhouette of its own icon with an ink "?", in the unfound-morph silhouette style, never glowing (open question 91); its rarity badge and rarity name; the name "???"; and a hint that names the habitat and the area where the treasure is found, then where to look by rarity, based on B6 (issues #106 and #113; open questions 90, 188, and 287). The rarity hints are:
    - Common: "Look near the main swim path!"
    - Rare: "Look off the main path!"
    - Epic: "Look in a cave or log, or past the current!" (issue #75, for the pond's hollow log; open question 150). It is unchanged for Area 3, whose root arch counts as a cave (open question 166).
    - Legendary: "Break a Dash pot with Tail Dash!"

    The hint is the habitat's name and the area's label, each followed by a colon, then the rarity hint with its first letter lower-cased (open questions 188 and 287), for example "Canal: Area 1: look near the main swim path!", "Lily pad pond: Area 2: look off the main path!", "Lily pad pond: Area 3: look in a cave or log, or past the current!", and "Chinampa gardens: Area 5: look near the main swim path!". The habitat and area words are the same as on the area map's habitat tabs (N2; open question 284). An owner decision on issue #113 puts the habitat first, which replaces issue #106's area-first order. Owner decisions on issue #106 (2026-10-22) that still hold: the hint names the area even when that area is not unlocked yet; and found treasures are unchanged. The area comes from the area data, never from the treasure's id: it is the first area, in area-table order, whose layout holds the treasure. A Dream colour's area is the area of its Paint shell (open question 189). An entry that no area holds shows its rarity hint alone; no shipped treasure does. The epic #32 rules for new areas (the predator ladder, the level layout ladder, the area shape rule, and the habitat milestones) do not apply, because only the chest's text changes.

    Paint shells use the same hint for their rarity. The chest lists the whole catalogue, the canal, then the pond, then Area 3, then Area 4, then Area 5 (B3): 25 Decorations, 31 Wearables, and 16 Dream colours (issue #103). An unfound Wearable shows its slot but not its perk. An unfound Dream colour shows a plain swatch silhouette with a "?" and keeps the label "Dream colour — not found in nature". Found and unfound are never told apart by colour alone (X2): the "?" and the "???" carry it. Banking a treasure fills its silhouette in the next time the Treasure Chest opens. The Treasure Chest also shows "X of Y found" for each category.

    Under each section heading, on every page of that section, a section line says what its items are and where to use them, in ink 18 pixel text (open question 99):
    - Decorations: "Things to put in your tank. Open the tank's decorate mode to place them."
    - Wearables: "Clothes for your axolotls. Each one gives a perk on trips. Dress up in Restyle."
    - Dream colours: "Magic paints from Paint shells. Not found in nature! Put one on in Restyle."

    Where the full line leaves no room for one whole row, a short line shows instead: "Place them in decorate mode.", "Wear them in Restyle for perks.", and "Magic paints. Use in Restyle." (open question 92). Where the short line still leaves no room for one whole row, the "Treasure Chest" title hides, as the Collection Book's does (C14): the heading moves up to the title's place and the list gains 47 pixels (open question 190). Text never shrinks (X5). The chest tries the full lines with the title, then the short lines with the title, then the short lines without the title. With the area in the hint, the tallest row, an unfound Dream colour, is 137.5 pixels at 460 x 320 and 320 x 440 (it was about 115). At 460 x 320 the list is 76.5 pixels with the full lines, 121.5 pixels with the short lines and the title, and 168.5 pixels with the short lines and no title; at 320 x 440 it is 75.5, 120.5, and 167.5 pixels. Measured on every supported canvas, the title hides only at 320 x 440, 340 x 440, 360 x 440, and 460 x 320. The short lines show at those canvases and at 460 x 340, 480 x 320, 480 x 340, 520 x 320, and 520 x 340, as before, and now also at 320 x 480, 375 x 440, 460 x 360, and 460 x 375, with the title shown. Every section shows at least one whole row per page at 460 x 320 and 320 x 440. The Treasure Chest has no new buttons.
12. **C12** The Collection Book shows the eight real morphs only: Leucistic, Wild, Golden albino, White albino, Copper, Piebald, Axanthic, and Melanoid. A found morph shows its picture, name, a distinct badge shape, and a fun-fact card. An unfound morph shows a friendly silhouette, "???", and a hint ("Rescue me in the canal!"). Found and unfound are never told apart by colour alone. Dream colours are never shown as morphs. They are listed in a separate section, each labelled "Dream colour — not found in nature".
13. **C13** Fun facts are true, short, and written for ages 8 to 12. Each is checked against a reputable source before release. Examples:
    - Leucistic: pale pink with dark eyes; not an albino.
    - Wild: natural dark, speckled colouring with shiny gold flecks.
    - Golden albino: no dark pigment; golden with light eyes.
    - White albino: looks a lot like Leucistic, but with pink eyes.
    - Copper: gets browner with age until it is copper all over.
    - Piebald: white with dark patches that darken with age.
    - Axanthic: has no yellow colour, so it is dark grey all over.
    - Melanoid: extra dark, with no shiny flecks.

    Fact cards are held to the same rule. The plastic worm fact card (N3, issue #75) has the title "Plastic is not food" and the body "Bits of plastic in the water can look like food. Animals sometimes eat them by mistake, and it makes them feel sick. Keeping rubbish out of the water helps." Source: NOAA Marine Debris Program, <https://marinedebris.noaa.gov/why-marine-debris-problem/ingestion> (open question 147).

    The heron fact card (N3, issue #98) has the title "Meet the heron" and the body "Herons are tall birds that live around Xochimilco's canals. They stand very still and watch the water for food. Axolotls are safer down deep, away from the surface." Sources: National Audubon Society, Guide to North American Birds, Great Egret, <https://www.audubon.org/field-guide/bird/great-egret> ("Forages mostly by standing or walking in shallow water, waiting for fish to come near ... then catching them with rapid thrust of bill"), and Great Blue Heron, <https://www.audubon.org/field-guide/bird/great-blue-heron> ("Forages mostly by standing still or walking very slowly in shallow water, waiting for fish to swim near, then striking with a rapid thrust of the bill."). Both were checked on 2026-10-22 (issue #111): they confirm the wading heron's behaviour, standing still and then striking fast, and the card's text does not change. The quotes were read through a page summarizer, so the owner is to check their exact wording on the pages. The owner is also to review the sources, and to check that herons live around Xochimilco's canals against a Xochimilco bird list (for example CONABIO or the Parque Ecológico de Xochimilco), before release; that bird list was not checked.

    The ghost net fact card (N3, issue #76) has the title "Lost fishing nets" and the body "Old fishing nets that get lost in the water can tangle up animals. People pull them out of canals and lakes to keep animals safe." (the owner's text, word for word). Sources: NOAA Marine Debris Program, "Increasing the Efficiency of Locating and Removing Ghost Nets in the Upper Great Lakes", <https://marinedebris.noaa.gov/removal/increasing-efficiency-locating-and-removing-ghost-nets-upper-great-lakes> (ghost nets can keep trapping fish, other animals, and waterfowl; a ghost net removal program in Lake Superior); and NOAA Marine Debris Program, "Removing and Preventing Derelict Fishing Gear and Large Debris Nationwide", <https://marinedebris.noaa.gov/removal/removing-and-preventing-derelict-fishing-gear-and-large-debris-nationwide> (lost gear keeps tangling and trapping animals; removal from the ocean and waterways). The pages support lakes and waterways, but neither names canals, so the owner is to verify the sources and the word "canals" before release (open question 199).

    The garter snake fact card (N3, issue #103) has the title "Meet garter snakes" and the body "Garter snakes live near water in Mexico and are good swimmers. They hunt small animals near the surface. Axolotls are safer down deep." The title was "Meet the garter snake"; the owner shortened it (owner decision on issue #103, comment 6375562228) so it stays on one line and the card fits 320-tall landscape screens. The body is unchanged. Sources, checked 2026-10-23: Manjarrez, Pacheco-Tinoco and Venegas-Barrera, "Intraspecific variation in the diet of the Mexican garter snake Thamnophis eques", PeerJ 5:e4036 (2017), <https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj.4036> (snakes sampled on the Mexican Plateau; they forage along the shore, an attack may be a sudden lunge across the surface toward prey, and their prey is mostly fish, then leeches, earthworms, frogs, and tadpoles); Manjarrez, Rivas-Gonzalez, Venegas-Barrera and Moyaho, "Visual Detection of Speckles in the Fish Xenotoca variata by the Predatory Snake Thamnophis melanogaster in Water of Different Turbidity", PLoS ONE 10(6):e0129429 (2015), <https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pone.0129429> (T. melanogaster is endemic to the highlands of central Mexico and forages underwater); and The Reptile Database, Thamnophis melanogaster, <https://reptile-database.reptarium.cz/species?genus=Thamnophis&species=melanogaster> (its distribution lists Distrito Federal, now Mexico City). Owner to verify (open question 243): "good swimmers", because the sources say they forage underwater, not how well they swim; "Axolotls are safer down deep", which is the game's rule (the snake strikes from the surface), not checked against a source; and garter snakes in Xochimilco itself, not checked.

    The water hyacinth fact card (N3, issue #103) has the title "Water hyacinth" and the body "Water hyacinth is a floating plant that grows very fast. In Xochimilco it can cover the canals and block the light. People clear it out to keep the water healthy." Sources, checked 2026-10-23: Carrion, Ponce-de Leon, Cram, Sommer, Hernandez and Vanegas (UNAM), "Aprovechamiento potencial del lirio acuatico (Eichhornia crassipes) en Xochimilco para fitorremediacion de metales", Agrociencia 46(6), 2012, <https://www.scielo.org.mx/scielo.php?script=sci_arttext&pid=S1405-31952012000600007> (a pest in the canals of the Xochimilco Protected Natural Area that spreads fast, forms mats, and cuts the light and the oxygen in the water); IUCN SSC Invasive Species Specialist Group, Global Invasive Species Database, Eichhornia crassipes, <https://www.iucngisd.org/gisd/species.php?sc=70> (populations can double in as little as 12 days, and the mats shade out native plants); Excelsior, "Arranca limpieza de canales en Xochimilco", 2020-08-31, <https://www.excelsior.com.mx/comunidad/arranca-limpieza-de-canales-en-xochimilco/1403028> (borough crews clear water weeds from the canals by hand, with harvesters where the water hyacinth is thickest); and LOS40 Mexico, "Con hechos, la Alcaldia Xochimilco recupera sus canales y genera empleos temporales para la comunidad", 2025-06-27, <https://los40.com.mx/2025/06/27/con-hechos-la-alcaldia-xochimilco-recupera-sus-canales-y-genera-empleos-temporales-para-la-comunidad/> (the borough removed more than 200 cubic metres). Owner to verify (open question 243): the clearing sources are news reports, not a CONABIO or borough page.

    The danger pass (issue #117) adds four cards, for the common carp, the punting pole, the tadpole, and the freshwater shrimp. No shipped area has any of them yet, so none is in the Collection Book yet (C14); each shows the first time it is met, with play paused, like A21. Their sources were checked on 2026-10-24 by web research only (the game fetches nothing), with source comments in `src/logic/factCards.ts`; no source is invented.

    The common carp fact card (N3; open question 254) has the title "Meet the carp" and the body "Common carp are big fish that people brought to Xochimilco. They dig in the mud for food and make the water cloudy. They also eat axolotl eggs." Sources: Zambrano, Valiente and Vander Zanden, "Food web overlap among native axolotl (Ambystoma mexicanum) and two exotic fishes: carp (Cyprinus carpio) and tilapia (Oreochromis niloticus) in Xochimilco, Mexico City", Biological Invasions 12(9):3061-3069 (2010), doi:10.1007/s10530-010-9697-8, read as the FAO AGRIS record, <https://agris.fao.org/search/en/records/65dfea8f0f3e94b9e5dd96ab> (carp and tilapia were brought to Xochimilco for aquaculture more than 20 years earlier and now dominate it), for "people brought to Xochimilco"; IUCN SSC Invasive Species Specialist Group, Global Invasive Species Database, Cyprinus carpio, <https://www.iucngisd.org/gisd/species.php?sc=60> (adults feed by "sucking up mud from the bottom"; feeding "churns up the sediments on the bottom of the water", "resulting in increased siltation and bioturbidity"), for "dig in the mud for food and make the water cloudy"; and National Geographic Kids, "Axolotl", <https://kids.nationalgeographic.com/animals/amphibians/facts/axolotl> (carp and tilapia "compete with axolotls for food and also eat axolotl eggs"), and The Wildlife Society, "Creating refuges for axolotls in Mexico City", 2021-12-06, <https://wildlife.org/creating-refuges-for-axolotls-in-mexico-city/> ("carp will feed on axolotl eggs"), for "They also eat axolotl eggs". Owner to verify: the egg claim rests on these two secondary sources, and no primary study of carp eating axolotl eggs in Xochimilco was found; "big fish" is not checked against a size source.

    The punting pole fact card (N3; open question 257), id `punting-pole`, has the title "Trajineras" and the body "Trajineras are bright, flat-bottomed boats on the canals of Xochimilco. A boater pushes each one along with a long pole that reaches down to the mud." Sources: Alex Zaragoza, NPR, "In Mexico City, A Technicolor Borough Serves Up Tasty Street Foods - On Water", 2017-01-02, read as the KPBS reprint, <https://www.kpbs.org/news/2017/01/02/in-mexico-city-a-technicolor-borough-serves-up> ("Xochimilco's famous trajineras - vibrantly painted barges"; a chalupa, a smaller canal boat, is steered "by pushing against the canal floor with a long wooden pole"); Sarah Freeman, National Geographic, "This fragile wetland is dying. Tour boats could be its unlikely savior.", 2022-01-25, <https://www.nationalgeographic.com/travel/article/nanobubbles-tour-boats-could-rescue-endangered-mexican-wetland> ("colorful tour boats known as trajineras, wend along a sun-dappled canal"; "Remeros still make barge poles as their ancestors did"; dead algae "settles at the bottom of the canal as sediment"); Ciudades Patrimonio de Mexico, "Xochimilco", <https://ciudadespatrimonio.mx/xochimilco/?lang=en> ("trajineras ornamentadas"; the chinampas are "anclados al fondo pantanoso", the marshy bottom); Wikipedia, "Xochimilco", <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xochimilco> (Xochimilco made "rafts, canoes and trajineras, pushed along the shallow waters by a pole"); and Wikipedia, "Trajinera", <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trajinera> ("a traditional, flat-bottomed wooden rowing boat, suited to the conditions of Lake Xochimilco", cited there to Fodor's). Owner to verify: "flat-bottomed" rests on Wikipedia (its Fodor's source returned 403); the same Wikipedia page says a rower uses an oar, while the other sources describe poles; and "the mud" is the sources' canal floor, sediment, and marshy bottom. UNESCO World Heritage Centre, "Historic Centre of Mexico City and Xochimilco", <https://whc.unesco.org/en/list/412>, returned 403 and was not read.

    The tadpole fact card (H4; open question 276) has the title "Meet the tadpole" and the body "Tadpoles are baby frogs. They swim by wiggling their tails, and they grow legs as they get bigger. Axolotls hunt small water animals like tadpoles." Sources: The Australian Museum, "Interactive: Frog life cycle, from egg to adult", <https://australian.museum/learn/teachers/learning/frog-life-cycle/> ("Most tadpoles hatch from eggs as fully aquatic larvae"; "They breathe through gills and use a long tail to swim"; "Hind legs emerge first, followed by front legs"), for the first two sentences; and National Geographic Kids, "Axolotl", <https://kids.nationalgeographic.com/animals/amphibians/facts/axolotl> ("Axolotls eat whatever they can get their jaws on, including mollusks, worms, insect larvae, crustaceans, tadpoles"), and Animal Diversity Web, Ambystoma mexicanum (Amy Majchrzak), <https://animaldiversity.org/accounts/Ambystoma_mexicanum/> ("axolotls will eat anything that they can catch"; its animal foods list includes amphibians), for the third.

    The freshwater shrimp fact card (H4; open question 276) has the title "Freshwater shrimp" and the body "Freshwater shrimp are tiny animals with many legs. When something comes near, they flick their tails and zip backward. Axolotls hunt small water animals like these." Sources: The Australian Museum, "Freshwater Shrimp" (Paratya australiensis), <https://australian.museum/learn/animals/crustaceans/freshwater-shrimp/> ("five pairs of long thin legs"; it "uses a flick of the tail to move quickly and escape from predators"); Derwent Estuary Program, "Species of the Derwent", <https://www.derwentestuary.org.au/species/shrimp> (the glass shrimp Paratya australiensis has "a vigorous backward-flicking escape response"; "Freshwater shrimp live in freshwater creeks and streams"), for "zip backward"; and San Diego Zoo Wildlife Alliance Library, "Diet & Feeding - Axolotl (Ambystoma mexicanum) Fact Sheet", <https://ielc.libguides.com/sdzg/factsheets/axolotl/diet> (wild adults eat "Small crustaceans"), and National Geographic Kids, "Axolotl", <https://kids.nationalgeographic.com/animals/amphibians/facts/axolotl> (axolotls eat "crustaceans"), for the third sentence. Owner to verify: "tiny" (the Australian Museum lists a size of 7 cm); and the shrimp sources are about Australian species, not species of Xochimilco.
14. **C14** The Collection Book also holds fact cards, in this order:
    - One fact card for each hazard and predator in the area data, then one for each timing element type that carries a fact card, then one for each mobile snack type, area by area in area-table order (N2): within an area its hazard and predator cards come first (a moving hazard's after the still hazards'), then its element cards, then its snack type cards, the tadpole's before the shrimp's (issue #117; open question 276). Each is found once the player has met that hazard, predator, element, or snack type (A21, N3, H4; open questions 119 and 229). A new card joins the book only when some area in the area table has its hazard, predator, element, or snack type, so the common carp, punting pole, tadpole, and freshwater shrimp cards (C13) join it with the first phase 2 issue that adds each one to an area; until then the book keeps its 9 cards (open question 266). Today that is the tilapia fact card, then the plastic worm fact card, then the heron fact card, then the ghost net fact card, then the garter snake fact card, then the water hyacinth fact card (C13; issue #103). A found card shows a picture only when it has Book art; today only the tilapia's does, and the heron, ghost net, garter snake, and water hyacinth cards have none (open question 159). An unfound card shows "???" and a hint that names the habitat of the first area, in area order, where its hazard or predator appears: "Explore the canal to find this card." for the tilapia, "Explore the lily pad pond to find this card." for the plastic worm and for the heron, whose first area, Area 3, is in the lily pad pond habitat, and "Explore the chinampa gardens to find this card." for the ghost net (issue #76) and for the garter snake and the water hyacinth, whose first area, Area 5, is in the chinampa gardens habitat (issue #103). The ghost net card is found the first time the axolotl comes within 100 pixels of a ghost net's edge, outside the Home Spot (N3). The garter snake card is found the first time the axolotl comes within 180 pixels of a garter snake's head, and the water hyacinth card the first time it comes within 180 pixels of a mat pair's sweep, each outside the Home Spot (N3).
    - The "Boy or girl?" fact card, found from the start, with no picture. Its text: "Grown-up boy axolotls are longer and slimmer, with a bigger bump by the back legs. Girls are rounder and plumper." Source: Animal Diversity Web (University of Michigan), <https://animaldiversity.org/accounts/Ambystoma_mexicanum/>. Supporting: the Royal Veterinary College axolotl care sheet. The boy's longer tail fin (F2) is a drawn cue only, not a fact on the card.
    - Two refuge fact cards (N2, issue #74), each found in the refuge, with its own unfound hint and no Book art (open questions 182 and 183):
        - "Axolotl refuges", found on the first visit to the refuge. Its body: "In Xochimilco, people make safe canals for axolotls next to chinampa gardens. Filters of volcanic rock let water in but keep big fish like tilapia out." Unfound hint: "Visit the refuge to find this card." Sources: Tec Science (Tec de Monterrey), "How to Save the mexican Axolotl? Start by Restoring Their Home", 2025-01-31, <https://tecscience.tec.mx/en/biotechnology/how-to-save-the-axolotl>; and The Wildlife Society, "Creating refuges for axolotls in Mexico City", 2021-12-06, <https://wildlife.org/creating-refuges-for-axolotls-in-mexico-city/> (Zambrano et al., Aquatic Conservation, doi:10.1002/aqc.3520).
        - "Canal clean-ups", found the first time cleared trash is on screen while it is not found: after a clean-up plays, or on opening a refuge that already has a cleared batch, so older saves find it too. Its body: "People in Xochimilco pull rubbish, like plastic bottles, out of the canals. Keeping the water clean helps keep axolotls safe." Unfound hint: "Beat an area, then visit the refuge to find this card." Sources: EU Delegation to Mexico, "Clean-up activity in Xochimilco, Mexico City, as part of the #EUBeachCleanup campaign", 2021-09-27, <https://www.eeas.europa.eu/delegations/mexico/clean-activity-xochimilco-mexico-city-part-eubeachcleanup-campaign_en>; and AFP via The Jakarta Post, "Mexican kayaker on mission to clean up floating gardens", 2022-02-04, <https://www.thejakartapost.com/culture/2022/02/04/mexican-kayaker-on-mission-to-clean-up-floating-gardens>.
        - On a first visit both may show, "Axolotl refuges" first. The owner is to verify both cards' sources before release (open question 187). Their ids are saved with the hazards met (D2).
    - So there are 9 fact cards today, in this order: the tilapia, the plastic worm, the heron, the ghost net, the garter snake, the water hyacinth, "Axolotl refuges", "Canal clean-ups", and "Boy or girl?" (issue #103). The section heading reads "Fact cards: N of 9 found". The ghost net, garter snake, and water hyacinth cards' ids are saved with the hazards met (D2), with no save change (ADR 0012's additive rule).
    - Fit (issues #75 and #98; open questions 151 and 173): on a trip, a fact card's title wraps at a tight-padding width, the canvas width less twice the margin and the tight padding, from 120 to 420 pixels (404 pixels at 460 wide), so the title always shows. The body first wraps at the body width. Only when the body does not fit the canvas height there does it wrap at the title's tight-padding width instead, which takes fewer lines on canvases under 484 pixels wide. Today only the heron card and the water hyacinth card (issue #103) at 320 x 440 do so. The heron body takes 9 lines at 240 pixels, which does not fit, and 8 lines at 264 pixels, which fits, using 397 of the 408 pixels of height. In the Collection Book, the "Collection Book" title hides only on a featured page whose entry does not fit under it; the heading then moves up and the list gains 47 pixels. Measured, it hides only on the found plastic worm card's page, at 320 x 440, 460 x 320, and 460 x 340, on the found heron card's page, at 320 x 440, 340 x 440, 460 x 320, 460 x 340, 480 x 320, and 480 x 340, and, since issue #103, on the found water hyacinth card's page, at 320 x 440, 460 x 320, 460 x 340, 480 x 320, and 480 x 340.
    - Fit of the refuge cards (issue #74): they add 2 pages to the Collection Book, so it has 28 pages at 1 row per page and 17 at 5 rows per page. The list of pages where the title hides does not change: the refuge cards keep the "Collection Book" title on every supported canvas. On their fact card, the closest fits are: "Axolotl refuges" 0.5 pixels to spare at 460 x 360 and 480 x 360 (padding 24), and 11 pixels at 320 x 440 (padding 12); "Canal clean-ups" 5.5 pixels at 460 x 340, 480 x 340, and 520 x 340.
    - Fit of the ghost net card (issue #76): on a trip, its title and body fit every supported canvas, the body at the body width; the heron at 320 x 440 is still the only card whose body wraps at the tight-padding width. In the Collection Book, the ghost net card, found or not, keeps the "Collection Book" title at 460 x 320 and 320 x 440, and the list of pages where the title hides does not change. With Poppy red and the ghost net card, the Collection Book has 30 pages at 1 row per page and 18 at 5 rows per page.
